L5: Table 1. Application Information
L6: Application type	BLA
Application number(s)	761286
Priority or standard	Priority
Submit date(s)	10/24/2022
Received date(s)	10/24/2022
PDUFA goal date	6/24/2023
Division/office	Division of Neurology I (DNI)
Review completion date	6/23/2023
Established/proper name	rozanolixizumab-noli
(Proposed) proprietary name	Rystiggo
Pharmacologic class	Neonatal FcRn blocker
Other product name(s)	Also referred to as UCB7665
Applicant	UCB, Inc
Dosage form(s)/formulation(s)	Solution for injection. Each 2 mL single-dose vial contains 280 mg of rozanolixizumab-noli, histidine (2.10 mg), L-histidine hydrochloride monohydrate (9.74 mg), proline (57.56 mg), polysorbate 80 (0.60 mg), and water for injection, USP with a pH of 5.6.
Dosing regimen	The recommended dosage is administered as a subcutaneous infusion once weekly for 6 weeks.
Body Weight of Patient	Dose	Volume to be Infused
Less than 50 kg	420 mg	3 mL
50 kg to less than 100 kg	560 mg	4 mL
100 kg and above	840 mg	6 mL

Applicant-proposed indication(s)/population(s)	Treatment of generalized myasthenia gravis in adult patients who are anti-acetylcholine receptor (AChR) or anti-muscle-specific kinase (MuSK) antibody positive
SNOMED CT code for proposed indication disease term(s)1	91637004 Myasthenia Gravis
Regulatory action	Approval
Approved dosage (if applicable)	As above
Approved indication(s)/population(s) (if applicable)	For the treatment of generalized myasthenia gravis in adult patients who are anti-acetylcholine receptor (AChR) or anti-muscle-specific kinase (MuSK) antibody positive
SNOMED CT code for approved indication disease term(s)1	91637004 Myasthenia Gravis

L34: ## I. Executive Summary
L35: ## 1. Summary of Regulatory Action
L36: Union Chimique Belge (UCB) submitted a biologics license application (BLA) 761286 for rozanolixizumab. Rozanolixizumab is a neonatal fragment crystallizable receptor (FcRn) blocker for the treatment of adults with generalized myasthenia gravis (gMG) who are anti-acetylcholine receptor (AChR) or anti-muscle-specific kinase (MuSK) antibody positive.
L37: BLA 761286 was reviewed by a multidisciplinary review team that did not identify any issues that preclude approval. Each discipline has recommended approval. I, the signatory authority for this application, concur with those recommendations and agree that the benefit-risk assessment supports approval.
L38: The Applicant submitted results from a single adequate and well-controlled study (Study MG0003), with confirmatory evidence from pharmacodynamic/mechanistic data and knowledge of other drugs in class, that provide substantial evidence of effectiveness of rozanolixizumab for the indication and supports approval for the treatment of adults with gMG who are anti-acetylcholine receptor (AChR) or anti-muscle-specific kinase (MuSK) antibody positive. Study MG0003 was a double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicenter study that provided reliable and highly statistically significant (p&lt;0.001) evidence of an important patient-reported impact and clinical benefit on daily function on an acceptable primary outcome (i.e., change from baseline to day 43 on the Myasthenia Gravis-Specific Activities of Daily Living [MG-ADL]). Efficacy was further supported by a highly statistically significant result (p&lt;0.001) on a secondary endpoint using a validated direct physician assessment of motor function in gMG subjects (change from baseline on the Quantitative Myasthenia Gravis (QMG) score). Supplementary analyses supported the primary and secondary results. Overall, the highly persuasive results on a clinically meaningful endpoint, with support on the secondary endpoint of QMG and multiple supplementary analyses, make reliance on a single efficacy study in this rare disease, with confirmatory evidence from relevant pharmacodynamic/mechanistic data and knowledge of other drugs in the class, appropriate to support approval.
L39: The available safety data show that risks of rozanolixizumab are acceptable for its intended use. I concur that identified risks can be mitigated through labeling and further evaluated during routine and enhanced pharmacovigilance. The overall benefit-risk is favorable as described in the benefit-risk framework below. For detailed information supporting the basis for this approval, please refer to the detailed reviews included in this interdisciplinary assessment document and the product quality review.
L40: ## 2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L41: ## 2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework

L43: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of condition	Myasthenia gravis (MG) is a rare chronic autoimmune neuromuscular disease. A systematic review and metaanalysis of published literature estimated the worldwide prevalence of MG to be 12.4 people per 100,000 population (Salari et al. 2021). In the United States, the prevalence is estimated at 14 to 20 per 100,000 population equating to approximately 36,000 to 60,000 patients in the country (MGFA 2015). However, the prevalence in the real world may be higher as MG remains underdiagnosed. At disease onset, some patients experience isolated ptosis and diplopia known as ocular myasthenia gravis. Patients with generalized myasthenia gravis experience weakness in other distributions such as bulbar, axial, limb, and respiratory muscles.
MG is caused by antibodies that bind to functional proteins at the neuromuscular junction. A large majority of patients have detectable antibodies against the acetylcholine receptor (AChR; 80% of patients), whereas less than 10% of patients have antibodies against muscle-specific kinase (MuSK). Antibodies are not detected in 10 to 15% of patients. The anti-AChR antibodies cause damage of the postsynaptic membrane at the neuromuscular junction by activating the complement cascade and antigenic modulation. Anti-MuSK antibodies lead to a reduced density and impaired alignment of AChR in the postsynaptic membrane at the neuromuscular junction. The resulting loss of AChR
MG is a rare, serious, life-threatening disease that can cause disability due to muscle weakness and death due to respiratory failure. Different autoantibodies can result in different subgroups of myasthenia gravis with variable phenotypes and severity.
L44: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
impairs neuromuscular transmission, which manifest as fatigable muscle weakness.
Women are more often affected than men, with a female to male ratio of 3:1 for anti-AChR-MG and 9:1 for anti-MuSK-MG. Typically, MG affects young adult women (under 40 years of age) and older men (over 60 years of age), but it can occur at any age. Anti-MuSK-MG is predominant in females (>70% of patients) and symptom onset is earlier with a peak of incidence in the 3rd decade.
Typical symptoms of MG may include drooping of eyelids, double vision, inability to reflect emotion due to facial weakness, trouble in swallowing, breathing, and speaking, and muscle weakness in the neck, upper and lower limbs. Life-threatening bulbar and respiratory muscle weakness (MG crisis) occurs in 20% of patients with anti-AChR-MG that requires immediate emergency medical care. In contrast, up to 80% of patients with anti-MuSK-MG have acute onset of bulbar involvement with rapid deterioration, frequently progressing to respiratory failure. Atypical clinical features such as head drop related to neck extensor weakness, muscle atrophy, and absence of typical fluctuation in MG symptoms can make the diagnosis challenging in patients with anti-MuSK-MG.
The diagnosis of MG is based on the clinical presentation, the presence of autoantibodies (anti-AChR, anti-MuSK, and others), and neurophysiological tests such as repetitive nerve stimulation and single-fiber electromyography.

Current treatment options	Available treatments include acetylcholine esterase inhibitors such as pyridostigmine bromide, which is FDA-approved for MG. Immunosuppressive treatments such as prednisone, azathioprine, mycophenolate mofetil, tacrolimus, rituximab, and plasmapheresis and intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIg) are used off-label. Thymectomy is also a treatment option for some patients.
Intravenous complement inhibitor monoclonal antibodies, eculizumab (Soliris) and ravulizumab (Ultomiris), have
There remains a significant unmet clinical need for effective treatments for MG because not all patients are able to receive, tolerate, or adequately benefit from the currently available clinical treatments.
Corticosteroids, broadly recognized as a mainstay therapy for MG have a rapid action, are inexpensive, and usually result in benefit to patients with MG. However, patients often develop intolerable side effects. Nonsteroidal immunosuppressant treatments may have a delayed response and significant side

been FDA-approved for the treatment of patients with anti-AChR-gMG, although their drug labels carry a boxed warning for serious meningococcal infections and have an associated Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategy. More recently intravenous efgartigimod (Vyvgart), an Immunoglobulin G subclass 1 fragment crystallizable neonatal fragment crystallizable receptor has been FDA-approved for the treatment of patients with anti-AChR-gMG, with warnings and precautions for infections and hypersensitivity reactions.
There are no FDA-approved biologics for anti-MuSK-MG. Furthermore, the symptomatic treatment with acetylcholine esterase inhibitors may be associated with poor tolerance due to side effects in patients with anti-MuSK-MG. Steroids and nonsteroidal immunosuppressant drugs (azathioprine, mycophenolate, tacrolimus, methotrexate, and cyclosporine, rituximab) have been used in patients with anti-MuSK-MG; however, it is often challenging to achieve a long-term effective remission. About 10 to 15% of these patients do not respond to steroids or other immunosuppressant treatment.

Many patients have inadequate response to steroids and nonsteroidal immunosuppressant treatment, with persistent and disabling muscle weakness, and disease relapses which may progress to MG crisis. Plasma exchange and intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIg) are used in patients with acute severe MG as their effect is rapid but short-lived.

Benefit	The efficacy of rozanolixizumab was evaluated in Study MG0003, a 14-week, phase 3, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicenter (81 sites across 15 countries) study that enrolled 200 adults (\ge 18 years of age) with the anti-AChR or anti-MuSK seropositive generalized MG (gMG) who experienced moderate to severe symptoms (Myasthenia Gravis Foundation of America [MGFA II-IVa]) and were being considered for additional treatment such as IVIg or plasma exchange. Subjects were randomized and stratified by the presence of the anti-AChR or anti-MuSK autoantibodies. The subjects were randomized 1:1:1, including two weight-tier based dose arms of rozanolixizumab (approximately 7 mg/kg and 10 mg/kg bodyweight) and one placebo arm (0.9% sodium chloride aqueous solution or physiological saline, preservative free). The study had a 6-week treatment cycle of once
The 14-week randomized double-blind, placebo-controlled study of rozanolixizumab provided reliable and statistically persuasive evidence that rozanolixizumab can help patients with anti-AChR or anti-MuSK antibody positive gMG achieve a clinically meaningful improvement in the symptoms of the disease that affect their activities of daily living, motor function, and disease severity.
The study population of 16 subjects with anti-MuSK antibody positive gMG comprised 8% of the total 200 subjects based on the testing at baseline visit, which is consistent with the disease prevalence in the intended patient population (Gilhus et al. 2019). Despite the small number of subjects with anti-MuSK-gMG, there was a clear signal favoring rozanolixizumab for a clinically meaningful improvement in the symptoms of the

The primary efficacy endpoint was change from baseline to day 43 in MG-specific Activities of Daily Living (MG-ADL) score after the 6-week treatment cycle in the anti-AChR or anti-MuSK seropositive gMG subject population.
A key secondary endpoint was change from baseline to day 43 in the quantitative myasthenia gravis (QMG) score after the 6-week treatment cycle in the anti-AChR or anti-MuSK seropositive gMG subject population.
In Study MG0003, for all subjects with either anti-AChR-gMG or anti-MuSK-gMG, there was a statistically and clinically meaningful change favoring rozanolixizumab in the mean MG-ADL score (rozanolixizumab, -3.4 points for both doses versus placebo, -0.8 points; p<0.001).
For all subjects with either anti-AChR-gMG or anti-MuSK-gMG, there was also statistically and clinically meaningful change favoring rozanolixizumab in the mean quantitative myasthenia gravis score (rozanolixizumab, -5.4 and -6.7 points for \approx 7 mg/kg and \approx 10 mg/kg doses, respectively versus placebo, -1.9 points; p<0.001).

Because the sample size is small, the effectiveness of rozanolixizumab in patients 65 years of age and older is not adequately established based on the studies included in the BLA.
The proposed drug label states that rozanolixizumab should only be prepared and infused by a healthcare provider.
Compared to intravenous drug delivery, subcutaneous administration may help reduce patient treatment burden and drug delivery-related healthcare cost and resources use, and in general, it is preferred by both patients and healthcare providers.
The potential role of rozanolixizumab for the treatment of patients with seronegative MG and those with purely ocular MG or severe MG who had MG crisis or impending MG crisis is unclear because these patients were not evaluated in the clinical studies included in this BLA submission.

Risk and risk management	The safety database (Safety Pool S1; Studies MG0003, MG0004 [first 6 weeks of treatment], and MG0007) included 196 subjects with gMG who were anti-AChR antibody-positive or anti-MuSK antibody-positive exposed to at least one dose of rozanolixizumab. A total of 133 subjects completed more than 12 months in these studies. Of these 133 subjects, 100 (75.2%) subjects had at least 4 treatment cycles of rozanolixizumab, and 88 (66.1%) subjects had at least 5 treatment cycles, which is the maximum number of cycles possible per one year, as per the protocol. The minimum time to initiate subsequent treatment cycle as defined by the protocol in the open-label extension Study MG0007 was 63 days (9 weeks) from the start of the previous treatment cycle.
Safety Concerns
Treatment-emergent adverse event (TEAE) with fatal outcome: There were five deaths in the safety database of 196 subjects. All deaths occurred in the open-label extension Study MG0007. Three deaths occurred in the setting of infection. Two deaths attributed to coronavirus disease of 2019 (COVID-19) occurred in subjects receiving rozanolixizumab \approx10 mg/kg. One death was due to multilobar pneumonia in which no pathogen could be identified. Two deaths attributed to cardiac events occurred during the follow-up period or intermittent period between MG0004 and MG0007.
A serious adverse event (SAE) of aseptic meningitis occurred in 1 (0.005%) subject out of total 196 subjects treated with rozanolixizumab. The subject experienced headache, nuchal rigidity, diarrhea, nausea, vomiting, and fever 1.5 days after the first infusion of rozanolixizumab \approx10 mg/kg dose (MG0007). The safety of initiating subsequent treatment cycles sooner than 63 days (9 weeks) from the start of the previous cycle has not been established for rozanolixizumab.

Common TEAEs: In the phase 3 double-blind, placebo-controlled Study MG0003, the most frequently reported TEAEs (\ge 5\% of subjects in the rozanolixizumab arm and more frequently than placebo) were headache (up to 48%), pyrexia (up to 22%), infections such as upper respiratory tract infection (up to 12%) and herpes simplex (up to 6%), gastrointestinal disorders such as diarrhea (up to 25%), nausea (up to 12%), vomiting (up to 6%), and abdominal pain (up to 9%), injection site reactions (up to 9%), skin rash (up to 7%), and arthralgia and myalgia (up to 7%).

Because treatment with rozanolixizumab is associated with reductions in serum IgG levels and increased frequency of infections, a Warnings and Precautions statement in labeling will highlight that the safety of immunization with live-attenuated or live vaccines and the response to immunization with any vaccine is unknown during treatment with rozanolixizumab, and that patients should be vaccinated with age-appropriate vaccinations according to immunization guidelines before starting treatment with rozanolixizumab.
Because the risk of adverse outcomes in pregnancy has not been characterized, and because rozanolixizumab will be used in women of childbearing potential, a pregnancy outcomes study is recommended as a postmarketing requirement.

There were no severe or serious reactions. One subject discontinued rozanolixizumab due to pruritis.

The risk of serious outcomes such as infections in the postmarketing period, when patients are likely to be observed less frequently than clinical studies is unknown.
The risk of adverse outcomes in pregnancy and potential harm to fetus have not been characterized. The risk of adverse effects on the breastfed infant is unknown.
Because individuals with hepatitis B surface antigen and hepatitis C antibody, and those with tuberculosis or latent tuberculosis infection were not evaluated in the clinical studies, the risk of reactivation of viral hepatitis and tuberculosis with rozanolixizumab is unknown.

L54: ## 2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
L55: MG is a serious, life-threatening disease that can result in significant morbidity and even mortality. Although there are several treatments approved to treat patients with gMG, there remains a need for effective treatments because not all MG patients are able to receive, tolerate, or adequately benefit from the currently available clinical treatments. Currently, there are two FDA-approved C5 inhibitors (eculizumab and ravulizumab) and one neonatal fragment crystallizable receptor (FcRn) blocker (efgartigimod) for intravenous administration in the treatment of adult patients with gMG who are anti-AChR antibody positive. Rozanolixizumab is a subcutaneously administered, humanized immunoglobulin G subclass 4 monoclonal antibody that inhibits FcRn, thereby reducing the effective general IgG pool which includes MG-specific autoantibodies.
L56: The Applicant has demonstrated the efficacy of rozanolixizumab for the treatment of gMG in patients who have anti-AChR antibody or anti-MuSK antibody in Study MG0003. This study was a randomized double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicenter study that enrolled 200 subjects with gMG who were on stable dose of AChE inhibitors, corticosteroids, or nonsteroidal immunosuppressive drugs. Subjects received a 6-week treatment cycle of weekly subcutaneous infusions of either rozanolixizumab \approx7 mg/kg dose or rozanolixizumab \approx10 mg/kg dose or placebo, with efficacy assessed 43 days after the initiation of treatment. Subjects were followed for 8 weeks after the end of the treatment cycle. The primary and the key secondary endpoints were accepted by the Division as clinically meaningful endpoints, and were used in labeling for efgartigimod, ravulizumab, and eculizumab. The Applicant has demonstrated a statistically significant and clinically meaningful effect on the primary endpoint, change from baseline in the myasthenia gravis activities of daily living (MG-ADL) total score (patient-reported outcome [PRO]) and the key secondary endpoint, change from baseline in the quantitative myasthenia gravis (QMg) total score (clinician-reported outcome [ClinRO]) at day 43 after the initiation of the treatment cycle (p&lt;0.001). There was an improvement (decrease) in the MG-ADL least squares mean total score by 3.4 points in the subjects treated with rozanolixizumab at both dose levels compared with a decrease of 0.8 points in the placebo group. Efficacy was supported by improvement (decrease) in the QMG least squares mean total score by -5.4 and -6.7 points in the rozanolixizumab \approx7 mg/kg and \approx10 mg/kg dose, respectively compared with a decrease of 1.9 points in the placebo group.
L57: The Applicant is seeking an indication for the treatment of adults with gMG who are anti-AChR or anti-MuSK antibody positive. The phase 3 study (MG0003) of rozanolixizumab enrolled 179 (89.5%) subjects with anti-AChR-gMG and 21 (10.5%) subjects with anti-MuSK-MG, which is consistent with the disease prevalence (Gilhus et al. 2019). Although the data are limited by the number of subjects with anti-MuSK-MG, treatment with rozanolixizumab in this population resulted in improvement in the MG-ADL and QMG scores compared to placebo, supporting the clinical efficacy of rozanolixizumab in this rarer form of gMG.
L58: Study MG0003 was an adequate and well-controlled, multicenter study that provides reliable and persuasive evidence that rozanolixizumab benefits adult patients with gMG who are anti-AChR or anti-MuSK antibody positive to achieve a clinically meaningful improvement in the symptoms of MG that affect their activities of daily living. Consistent and distinct treatment effects on the
L59: MG-ADL and the QMG confirm that the observed treatment differences between rozanolixizumab and placebo were highly clinically meaningful and not due to chance. Study MG0003 was large given the size of the patient population. No single site provided an unusually large proportion of subjects, and no site was disproportionately responsible for the observed treatment effect. These findings were statistically robust to sensitivity analyses and generalizable across demographic subgroups of age, sex, body weight, and region of the world. Additionally, pharmacodynamic and mechanistic data relevant to the pathophysiology of MG, reduction in anti-AChR and anti-MuSK antibodies, provide confirmatory evidence to support approval. There is also existing evidence for the effectiveness of the FcRn blocking mechanism in the treatment of gMG with the approval of efgartigimod for the treatment of adults with gMG who are anti-AChR antibody positive (FDA approved in 2021) (Argenx 2021). Therefore, the study is capable of serving as a single adequate and well-controlled clinical study plus confirmatory evidence to establish substantial evidence of effectiveness of rozanolixizumab treatment in adult patients with gMG who are anti-AChR or anti-MuSK antibody positive.
L60: The safety database for rozanolixizumab was adequate for the intended population and proposed dosing regimen. The safety database for gMG was characterized in a single, double-blind, placebo-controlled study (MG0003) and two open-label extension studies: Studies MG0004 and MG0007 (to capture the safety of repeated cycles of treatment). Common adverse events included headache, infection, diarrhea, nausea, abdominal pain, hypersensitivity reactions, and local administration site reactions. Serious adverse events of infections, aseptic meningitis, headache, vomiting, and gastritis were reported in subjects treated with rozanolixizumab which required hospitalization and treatment discontinuation or interruptions. Safety findings observed during the study can be adequately addressed through labeling and through routine and enhanced pharmacovigilance. Warning and Precautions statements include infections, aseptic meningitis, hypersensitivity reactions, and vaccination-related concerns. Enhanced pharmacovigilance will be requested for serious adverse events related to infections, reactivation of latent tuberculosis and hepatitis B, and malignancies. A pregnancy outcomes study is recommended as a postmarketing requirement.
L61: Based on the review of all available efficacy and safety data, the benefits of rozanolixizumab outweigh the risks for the treatment of adult patients with gMG who are anti-AChR or anti-MuSK antibody positive. The availability of rozanolixizumab will provide a new and effective treatment option via weekly subcutaneous infusion in 6-week symptom-driven treatment cycles for anti-AChR-gMG and anti-MuSK-gMG.
L62: ## II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
L63: ## 3. Introduction
L64: Myasthenia gravis (MG) is a rare antibody-mediated autoimmune neuromuscular disease. The incidence and prevalence of MG varies across geographical regions. Patient with myasthenia gravis can be classified according to clinical features of the disease. Approximately two-thirds of patients will present with diplopia and ptosis and other symptoms restricted to the ocular muscles. Patients with generalized myasthenia gravis (gMG) experience weakness in other distributions such as bulbar, axial, limb, and respiratory muscles. Myasthenic crisis is a severe life-threatening presentation of gMG, which requires intensive care, ventilation support, and enteral feeding. A systematic review and metaanalysis of published literature estimated the worldwide prevalence of MG to be 12.4 people per 100,000 population (Salari et al. 2021). In the United States, the prevalence is estimated at 14 to 20 per 100,000 population equating to approximately 36,000 to 60,000 patients in the country (MGFA 2015). However, the prevalence in the real world may be higher as MG remains underdiagnosed.
L65: A majority (80%) of patients with gMG have detectable antibodies against acetylcholine receptor (AChR), the major cation channel at the postsynaptic membrane of the NMJ. About 1 to 10 percent of total gMG patients (30 to 50 percent of patients with anti-AChR antibody seronegative gMG) have antibodies against muscle-specific kinase (MuSK), which is responsible for clustering of the AChR at the postsynaptic membrane of the neuromuscular junction (NMJ) (Gilhus et al. 2019). The anti-AChR antibody response is polyclonal and activates the complement cascade upon antigen binding, leading to the formation of the membrane attack complex and damage of the postsynaptic membrane at the NMJ. The anti-AChR antibodies induce antigenic modulation, with increased internalization and destruction of the AChR through receptor crosslinking by bivalent antibodies. Most anti-MuSK antibodies are of the functionally monovalent IgG4 subclass. The anti-MuSK antibodies are unable to activate complement and induce antigenic modulation. These antibodies inhibit MuSK function and lead to reduced AChR density and alignment at the postsynaptic membrane. Anti-MuSK-MG is predominant in females (&gt;70% of patients) and symptom onset is earlier with a peak of incidence in the third decade. The disease has typically acute onset of bulbar involvement (up to 80% of patients) with rapid deterioration, frequently progressing to respiratory failure. These patients have a higher risk of MG crisis. Head drop related to neck extensor weakness, muscle atrophy, and absence of typical fluctuation in MG symptoms can make the diagnosis challenging.
L66: There remains a significant unmet clinical need for effective treatments for gMG because not all patients are able to receive, tolerate, or adequately benefit from the available clinical treatments. Currently, patients with gMG have an option of symptomatic treatment with acetylcholine esterase (AchE) inhibitors such as FDA-approved pyridostigmine bromide, immunosuppressive treatment (off-label) such as steroids and nonsteroidal immunosuppressants, and plasma exchange or intravenous immunoglobulin therapy (also off-label). However, patients often experience intolerable side effects. Up to 30% of patients have inadequate response to steroids and nonsteroidal immunosuppressant treatment, with persistent and disabling muscle weakness,
L67: and disease relapses which may progress to MG crisis. There are no FDA-approved biologics for patients with anti-MuSK-gMG. Three biologics, including eculizumab and ravulizumab, monoclonal antibodies inhibiting complement activation at the C5 protein, and efgartigimod, an immunoglobulin G subclass 1 (IgG1) fragment crystallizable (Fc) fragment targeting FcRn, have been approved for the treatment of patients with anti-AChR-gMG. Steroids and nonsteroidal immunosuppressant drugs (azathioprine, mycophenolate, tacrolimus, methotrexate, and cyclosporine, rituximab) have been used in patients with anti-MuSK-gMG; however, it is often challenging to achieve a long-term effective remission. About 10 to 15% of patients anti-MuSK-gMG do not respond to steroids or other immunosuppressant drugs.
L68: The pivotal Study MG0003 and the open-label Studies MG0004 and MG0007 enrolled adults with gMG who were either anti-AChR or anti-MuSK antibody positive. The studies excluded the seronegative individuals with gMG, those with purely ocular MG, and those with severe weakness affecting oropharyngeal or respiratory muscles, or who had MG crisis or impending crisis.
L69: For this biologics license application (BLA), the investigational drug rozanolixizumab (Rystiggo, UCB7665) is a recombinant, humanized anti-FcRn immunoglobulin G subclass 4 monoclonal antibody (IgG4p) monoclonal antibody. The proposed mechanism of drug action is FcRn inhibition by rozanolixizumab leads to enhanced degradation of immunoglobulin G (IgG), with the aim to reduce pathogenic anti-AChR and anti-MuSK MG-specific autoantibodies. The Applicant's proposed indication for rozanolixizumab is the treatment of gMG in adult patients who are anti-AChR or anti-MuSK antibody positive.
L70: Study MG0003 was a phase 3, multinational, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study of rozanolixizumab for the treatment of generalized myasthenia gravis. In the study, subjects were treated for 6-weeks, with once weekly infusions of the investigational product. The total study duration was 14 weeks which included an 8-week observational period. In the open-label Study MG0007, subjects received additional treatment cycles separated by a variable intertreatment length of time depending on a subject's clinical response to rozanolixizumab.
L71: The review team identified three key review issues that impact the overall review of rozanolixizumab and the product labeling.

L108: ## 5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics, and Clinical Pharmacology
L109: ## 5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential Effectiveness
L110: The neonatal Fc receptor (FcRn) is a cell surface receptor located on a wide variety of cell types including endothelial cells and hematopoietic cells. This receptor binds endogenous IgG and albumin and prevents their endosomal degradation by releasing them back into circulation. This process of IgG “recycling” results in the observed long half-life of IgG in vivo (IgG t_{1/2} estimated to be 25 days or greater).
L111: UCB7665 (rozanolixizumab), a humanized IgG4 monoclonal antibody directed against the human FcRn, is intended to prevent recycling of IgG, but not albumin. By preventing IgG
L112: recycling, UCB7665 is expected to reduce the level of circulating autoantibodies thought to be involved in the pathogenesis of MG. In vitro and in vivo proof of concept studies were conducted to characterize the pharmacological activity of UCB7665 in cynomolgus monkeys and transgenic mice.
L113: ## **In Vitro**
L114: In BIAcore (i.e., plasmon surface resonance) assays, the affinity (K_D) of UCB7665 for human and monkey FcRn were 55 and 57 pM, respectively, at a pH of 7.4 and 44 and 42 pM, respectively, at a pH of 6.0. In Madin-Darby canine kidney (MDCK) cells that expressed human or monkey FcRn, the K_D was 0.43 and 1.03 nM, respectively. Additional studies in the same cell lines indicated inhibition of IgG recycling by human and monkey FcRN and inhibited transcytosis by human FcRn.
L115: ## **In Vivo**
L116: UCB7665 was tested in a transgenic mouse model (hFcRnTg) in which the human FcRn is expressed. Single doses of 0, 10, 30, or 100 mg/kg were administered by intravenous (IV) injection 1 day after pre-administration of human IgG. Results indicated a dose-dependent increase in human IgG clearance 24 hours postdose that was sustained until the end of the study (i.e., 192 hours postdose). No significant changes in albumin levels were detected relative to control.
L117: In the general toxicity studies in monkeys in which subcutaneous doses of up to \approx150 mg/kg UCB7665 were administered every 3 days for up to 26 weeks, pharmacodynamic effects consisted of reductions in IgG levels with no effects on immunoglobulin A or immunoglobulin M. Albumin was also reduced at the highest doses due to steric blocking of the albumin binding portion of FcRn.
L118: ## 5.2. Clinical Pharmacology/Pharmacokinetics
L119: **Table 5. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics**
L120: Characteristic	Drug Information
Pharmacologic Activity
Established pharmacologic class (EPC)	Rozanolixizumab is a recombinant, humanized antineonatal Fc receptor (FcRn) IgG4P monoclonal antibody.
Mechanism of action	Rozanolixizumab decreases serum IgG concentration, including anti-AChR- and anti-MuSK-specific pathogenic IgG by inhibiting the binding of IgG to FcRn (FcRn normally protects IgG from intracellular degradation and recycles IgG back to the cell surface)
Active moieties	Rozanolixizumab
QT prolongation	Dedicated QTc prolongation study was not conducted. Rozanolixizumab is a human IgG4P antibody fragment with a molecular weight of 147 kDa, and no direct ion channel effects are expected.
General Information
Bioanalysis	The plasma concentrations (in studies UP0018, TP0001 and MG0002) of rozanolixizumab were determined by validated enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay (ELISA) methods and redeveloped for studies CIDP01 and UP0060. The ELISA method was validated in compliance with the standards set forth in the FDA Bioanalytical Method Validation guidance.
Healthy subjects versus patients	Clinically relevant differences in PK are not expected between healthy subjects and patients.
Drug exposure at steady state following the therapeutic dosing regimen	At the end of treatment cycle (on day 43) following the sixth weekly dose of ≈7 mg/kg or ≈10 mg/kg, majority of plasma concentrations were BLQ from Study MG0003.
Range of effective dose(s) or exposure	Two dose-levels, ≈7 mg/kg and 10 mg/kg administered Q1W*6 were studied in phase 3 (MG0003) study.
Maximally tolerated dose or exposure	The highest evaluated dose in humans was a single dose of ≈20 mg/kg in adults with primary immune thrombocytopenia (ITP) (n=12) in study TP0001.
Dose proportionality	Rozanolixizumab exposure increased in a greater than dose proportional manner following a single subcutaneous dose in the range from ≈1 mg/kg to ≈20 mg/kg.
Accumulation	Plasma levels at Ctrough (1 week after dosing) are close to or below the limit of quantification (BLQ) in majority of patients, limiting the ability to calculate terminal half-life, and accumulation.
Time to achieve steady-state	Steady-state exposures of rozanolixizumab are expected to be achieved by the sixth weekly dose, i.e., within end of the treatment of the cycle (Q1W*6).
Bridge between to-be-marketed and clinical study formulations	The final-to-be-marketed (liquid formulation) was used in phase 3 clinical Study MG0003 (pivotal); therefore, no clinical PK-based bridging study is required.
L121: Characteristic	Drug Information
Absorption
Bioavailability	The absolute bioavailability for the \approx7 mg/kg dose was 14%; however, this value should be interpreted with caution given the target mediated drug disposition (TMDD) of rozanolixizumab.
Tmax	Approximately 2 days postinfusion.
Food effect (fed/fasted)	Rozanolixizumab is administered subcutaneously as an infusion (b) (4), and therefore food-effect is not relevant.
Geometric least square mean and 90% CI	
Distribution
Volume of distribution	Based on population PK analyses, the mean estimate of apparent volume of distribution was 6.6L.
Plasma protein binding	Plasma protein binding was not determined.
Drug as substrate of transporters	Rozanolixizumab is a human IgG4 monoclonal antibody and is unlikely to be affected by drug transporters; therefore, no transporter-mediated drug-interaction studies were conducted.
Elimination
Mass balance results	No dedicated mass balance study was conducted.
Clearance	Based on population PK analysis, the mean estimate for apparent CL/F was 0.89L/day.
Half-life	Precise half-life was not determined.
Metabolic pathway(s)	Rozanolixizumab is a human IgG4 monoclonal antibody and is expected to be predominantly catabolized by lysosomal degradation to small peptides and amino acids, which are expected to be excreted or recycled in the same manner as endogenous IgG.
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight	In Study MG0003, the Applicant evaluated a weight-tiered regimen in which subjects received once weekly (Q1W) fixed doses (based on specific bodyweight cut-offs), which equate \approx7 mg/kg or \approx10 mg/kg for 6 weeks (one treatment cycle). Refer to the pharmacometrics review for additional discussion on effect of body weight on PK and PD of rozanolixizumab
Age	No dose adjustment is needed based on age.
Renal impairment	No dose adjustment is needed in patients with renal impairment.
Hepatic impairment	No dose adjustment is needed in patients with hepatic impairment.
Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Perpetrator)
Inhibition/induction of metabolism	Rozanolixizumab is neither subject to CYP450 enzymes nor expected to interfere with cytokine levels. Therefore, no CYP450-mediated drug-interaction studies were conducted.
Inhibition/induction of transporter systems	Rozanolixizumab is a human IgG4 monoclonal antibody fragment and is unlikely to be affected by drug transporters; therefore, no transporter-mediated drug-interaction studies were conducted.
Interaction with other therapeutic moieties	Closely monitor for reduced effectiveness of medications that bind to the human FcRn (i.e., immunoglobulin products, monoclonal antibodies, or antibody derivatives containing the human Fc domain of the IgG subclass), when concomitant use is necessary. When concomitant long-term use of such medications is essential for patient care, consider discontinuing rozanolixizumab using alternative gMG medications. Other therapeutic proteins are unlikely to alter the binding of rozanolixizumab to FcRn receptor.

## Rystiggo (rozanolixizumab)
L123: Characteristic	Drug Information
Impact on immunizations	Vaccination with live-attenuated or live vaccines is not recommended during treatment with rozanolixizumab. Administer all age-appropriate vaccines according to immunization guidelines before initiation of treatment of rozanolixizumab.
Bioanalysis	Immunogenicity
Immunogenicity assessments were conducted using semihomogenous bridging electro-chemiluminescence immunoassay (ECLIA) assay was used to determine levels of antidrug antibodies (ADA), and neutralizing capacity of the ADA.
Incidence	In Study MG0003 following 99 days after last (6th weekly) dose of rozanolixizumab treatment, the incidence of antidrug antibodies (ADA) was 49/133 (37%) and the incidence of neutralizing antibody was 28/49 (57%)). Upon repeating the 6-week dosing cycle for up to five additional cycles in Study MG0007, the antidrug antibody incidence was 44.4% (71/160) and the incidence of neutralizing antibodies was 69% (49/71).
Clinical impact	There's no clinically relevant impact of ADA on efficacy or safety of rozanolixizumab.

L125: ## 6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
L126: ## 6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential Effectiveness
L127: The Applicant conducted a phase 1 study (UP0018) which was a first in human, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, single ascending dose study in adult healthy volunteers. The subjects (randomized 3:1) received \approx 1 mg/kg, \approx 4 mg/kg, or \approx 7 mg/kg either IV or SC as a one-hour infusion. Pharmacokinetic (PK), pharmacodynamic (PD), and immunogenicity (total IgG reduction) data were collected. There was a dose-dependent decrease in total IgG levels. Further, the Applicant noted that the mean maximum percentage reduction in total IgG concentrations after single doses of IV and SC treatment of \approx 7 mg/kg was 49% and 44%, respectively. In another phase 1 study (UP0060) which was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study in adult healthy volunteers, three dose levels were studied: cohort 1 (\approx 4 mg/kg), cohort 2 (\approx 7 mg/kg), and cohort 3 (\approx 10 mg/kg). Caucasian, Japanese, and Chinese subjects randomized to rozanolixizumab and placebo. Dose-dependent reductions in total IgG were observed in all ethnicities and there were no major differences across the ethnic populations for the \approx 7 mg/kg and \approx 10 mg/kg dose groups.
L128: The Applicant conducted a phase 2 study (MG0002), a multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, 2-arm, repeat-dose study in moderate to severe gMG patients. In dosing period 1, subjects received either placebo or \approx 7 mg/kg Q1W for 3 weeks. In dosing period 2, subjects were rerandomized to receive 3 doses of rozanolixizumab at weekly intervals at a dose of \approx 4 mg/kg or \approx 7 mg/kg, resulting in 4 treatment groups: placebo-rozanolixizumab \approx 7 mg/kg, placebo-rozanolixizumab \approx 4 mg/kg, rozanolixizumab \approx 7 mg/kg - \approx 7 mg/kg, and rozanolixizumab \approx 7 mg/kg - \approx 4 mg/kg. In dosing period 1, \approx 64\% reduction was observed in total IgG with the \approx 7 mg/kg dose compared to placebo. For subjects randomized to rozanolixizumab in both dosing periods, reduction in total IgG levels were higher compared to subjects randomized to placebo in dosing period 1. The \approx 7 mg/kg - \approx 7 mg/kg had slightly higher reduction in baseline IgG levels compared to \approx 7 mg/kg - \approx 4 mg/kg group (69% versus 64%). The Applicant also reported median maximum percent change of 65% to 75% reduction in pathogenic IgG (anti-AChR autoantibodies) in all dose groups.
L129: Based on the results from phase 1 and 2 studies and PK modeling and simulations, the Applicant selected \approx 7 mg/kg and \approx 10 mg/kg to be administered Q1W for 6 doses for the phase 3 study (MG0003) in moderate to severe gMG patients. Like Study MG0002, a mean maximum reduction of \approx 75\% in total IgG levels was observed. A median maximum reduction of 73% at \approx 7 mg/kg and 81% at \approx 10 mg/kg in anti-AChR autoantibodies was observed. The median maximum reduction of 37% at \approx 7 mg/kg and 56% at \approx 10 mg/kg in anti-MuSK autoantibodies was observed. These changes in PD response correlated with clinical efficacy response (reduction in MG-ADL scores) after six once-weekly doses.
L130: The Applicant proposed 2-fixed dose regimen: [REDACTED] (b) (4) and 420 mg for patients weighing [REDACTED] (b) (4) &lt;50 kg supported by popPK-PD-efficacy modeling. Although the model adequately characterizes the PK/PD profile at \approx 7- and \approx 10 mg/kg dose levels, a
L131: substantial proportion of rozanolixizumab plasma concentrations fall below the lower limit of quantification (LLOQ). In addition, the observed data for the 1 mg/kg dose suggests caution in relying on model predictions for low doses. Therefore, the clinical pharmacology review team expresses caution in the model's ability to accurately predict changes in total IgG and MG-ADL.
L132: Based on these considerations, the review team recommends 420 mg Q1W in patients &lt;50 kg and 560 mg Q1W in patients 50 kg to &lt;100 kg for 6 weeks (i.e., 1 treatment cycle), and 840 mg Q1W in patients 100 or more (discussed in detail in Section 6.3.3). This recommendation is consistent with the weight-tiered dosing regimen evaluated in Study MG0003 in gMG patients. The Applicant concurred with the review team's recommendation, including 840 mg rozanolixizumab dose in patients weighing 100 kg or more.
L133: Furthermore, the clinical pharmacology team has evaluated the proposed instructions by the applicant for delayed or missed dosing and finds them acceptable.
L134: ## 6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended to Demonstrate Efficacy
L135: ## 6.2.1. Results of Pooled Analyses
L136: Not applicable.
L137: ## 6.2.2. Study MG0003
L138: ## 6.2.2.1. Design, Study MG0003
L139: ## Overview and Objective
L140: Study MG0003 was a phase 3, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study of rozanolixizumab (UCB7665) in adult subjects with gMG who are anti-AChR or anti-MuSK antibody positive and experience moderate to severe symptoms (Myasthenia Gravis Foundation of America [MGFA] Class II-Iva).
L141: The primary objective was to evaluate the clinical efficacy of rozanolixizumab as assessed by the change from baseline to day 43 in MG-ADL score after the 6-week treatment cycle in the anti-AChR or anti-MuSK seropositive gMG subject population.
L142: The secondary objectives are listed below:
L143: - To evaluate the efficacy of rozanolixizumab as assessed by the change from baseline to day 43 in the myasthenia gravis composite (MG-C) score in the anti-AChR or anti-MuSK seropositive gMG subject population
- To evaluate the efficacy of rozanolixizumab as assessed by the change from baseline to day 43 in the QMG score in the anti-AChR or anti-MuSK seropositive gMG subject population
L144: - To evaluate the efficacy of rozanolixizumab as assessed by the change from baseline to day 43 in the MG Symptoms PRO Domain “muscle weakness fatigability” score in the anti-AChR or anti-MuSK seropositive gMG subject population
- To evaluate the efficacy of rozanolixizumab as assessed by the change from baseline to day 43 in the MG Symptoms PRO Domain “physical fatigue” score in the anti-AChR or anti-MuSK seropositive gMG subject population
- To evaluate the efficacy of rozanolixizumab as assessed by the change from baseline to day 43 in the MG Symptoms PRO Domain “bulbar symptoms” score in the anti-AChR or anti-MuSK seropositive gMG subject population
L145: ## **Basic Study Design**
L146: Study MG0003 was a multicenter (n=81 sites), multinational (15 countries: Canada, Czech Republic, Denmark, France, Georgia, Germany, Hungary, Italy, Japan, Poland, Russian Federation, Serbia, Spain, Taiwan), randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, 3-arm study which included two active-dose arms (approximately 7 mg/kg and 10 mg/kg bodyweight), and one placebo arm (0.9% sodium chloride aqueous solution or physiological saline, preservative free), phase 3 study in adults (\ge 18 years of age) with the anti-AChR or anti-MuSK seropositive gMG who experience moderate to severe symptoms (Myasthenia Gravis Foundation of America [MGFA] Class II-Iva) and are being considered for additional treatment such as intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIg) or plasma exchange (PEX). Subjects were randomized and stratified by the presence of the anti-AChR or anti-MuSK autoantibodies. An independent data monitoring committee (IDMC) monitored the ongoing safety and efficacy of the study.
L147: The total study duration was up to 18 weeks and included a 4-week screening period and a 6-week treatment period followed by an 8-week blinded observation period. During the treatment period, rozanolixizumab or placebo were administered subcutaneously once a week for 6 weeks. All eligible study subjects who completed the observation period could be rerandomized into an open-label extension (OLE) study to either active dose arm 1 or 2. The study design is shown in Figure 1.
L148: Figure 1. Study MG0003 Design
L149: IVIg=intravenous immunoglobulin G; OLE=open-label extension; PEX=plasma exchange
L150: Source: BLA Submission Module 2.7.3 Figure 1-1, Page 15, dated October 18, 2022
L151: Abbreviations: BLA, biologics license application; IVIG, immunoglobulin; MG, myasthenia gravis; OLE, open-label extension; PEX, plasma exchange
L152: Rozanolixizumab was administered subcutaneously at doses \approx 7\text{ mg/kg} and \approx 10\text{ mg/kg} based on a body weight-tiered approach with five different fixed doses across four body weight tiers (Table 6). The Applicant has proposed a 2-fixed dosing strategy for rozanolixizumab in the draft label and has submitted subgroup analysis by weight tier and administered dose for the primary and secondary endpoints in the pivotal Study MG0003.

L158: Study subjects who experienced disease worsening during the 6-week treatment period (e.g., an increase of two points on the MG-ADL or three points on the QMG scale between two consecutive visits) were considered for rescue therapy (IVIg or PEX) at the discretion of the
L159: ## BLA 761286
## Rystiggo (rozanolixizumab)
L160: investigator. No further infusions of rozanolixizumab were administered after initiation of rescue therapy. These subjects completed all study visits including the 8-week observation period.
L161: Study subjects who completed the treatment period and required initiation of rescue therapy after they started the 8-week observation period were either eligible to receive IVIg or PEX or complete the end of study visit and immediately roll over into an OLE study where the subject received rozanolixizumab. L174: ## Primary Efficacy Endpoint for Study MG0003
L175: The primary efficacy endpoint for Study MG0003 was the change from baseline to day 43 in Myasthenia Gravis Activities of Daily Living (MG-ADL) score after the 6-week treatment cycle in the anti-AChR or anti-MuSK seropositive gMG subject population.
L176: The MG-ADL is a categorical subject-reported outcome scale (Table 8) that assesses the impact on daily function of eight signs or symptoms that are typically affected in gMG. Each item is assessed on a 4-point scale where a score of 0 represents normal function and a score of 3 represents loss of ability to perform that function (total score 0 to 24). Thus, higher scores indicate greater severity of symptoms. The MG-ADL is composed of items related to subjects' assessment of functional disability secondary to ocular (2 items), bulbar (3 items), respiratory (1 item), and gross motor or limb impairment (2 items).
L177: **Table 8. Myasthenia Gravis Activities of Daily Living (MG-ADL) Scale**
L178: Symptom	0	1	2	3	Score
(0, 1, 2, 3)
1. Talking	Normal	Intermittent slurring of nasal speech	Constant slurring or nasal, but can be understood	Difficult to understand speech	
2. Chewing	Normal	Fatigue with solid food	Fatigue with soft food	Gastric tube	
3. Swallowing	Normal	Rare episode of choking	Frequent choking necessitating changes in diet	Gastric tube	
4. Breathing	Normal	Shortness of breath with exertion	Shortness of breath at rest	Ventilator dependence	
5. Impairment of ability to brush teeth or comb hair	None	Extra effort, but no rest periods needed	Rest periods needed	Cannot do one of these functions	
6. Impairment of ability to arise from a chair	None	Mild, sometimes uses arms	Moderate, always uses arms	Severe, request assistance	
7. Double vision	None	Occurs, but not daily	Daily, but not constant	Constant	
8. Eyelid droop	None	Occurs, but not daily	Daily, but not constant	Constant	
MG-ADL total score (items 1-8)
L179: Source: (Muppidi et al. 2022).

L182: **Reviewer's Comments:** The Division previously agreed with the acceptability of the change from baseline on MG-ADL score as a primary endpoint. The MG-ADL is considered to assess clinically meaningful changes in patient function impacted by gMG. There is also regulatory precedent for the use of the MG-ADL as a clinical endpoint for gMG, as it was also used in the clinical studies that led to the approval of eculizumab, ravulizumab, and efgartigimod for gMG.
L183: ## Key Secondary Efficacy Endpoints for Study MG0003
L184: The key secondary endpoints are outlined in 6.2.2.1. These included the change from baseline to day 43 in QMG and MG-C scores after the 6-week treatment cycle in the anti-AChR or anti-MuSK seropositive gMG subject population.
L185: The Quantitative Myasthenia Gravis (QMG) is a physician assessment scoring system (Table 9) that consists of 13 items: ocular (2 items), facial (1 item), bulbar (2 items), gross motor (6 items), axial (1 item), and respiratory (1 item). These 13 items are quantitatively assessed and graded from 0 to 3, with 3 being the most severe, providing a total QMG score ranging from 0 to 39.

L189: Abbreviations: QMG, quantitative myasthenia gravis
L190: **Reviewer's Comments:** The Division previously agreed with the acceptability of the change from baseline on QMG scale as a key secondary endpoint. There is also regulatory precedent for the use of the QMG as a clinical endpoint for gMG, as it was also used in the clinical studies that led to the approval of eculizumab, ravulizumab, and efgartigimod for gMG. Although the QMG assesses exam findings of gMG that may be of uncertain clinical meaningfulness, it supports that changes observed on the MG-ADL are related to improvements in physical manifestations of the disease.
L191: The Myasthenia Gravis Composite (MG-C) is a 10-item subject-reported and physician assessment scoring system (Table 10). The MG-C is composed of items originating from other scales (i.e., QMG, manual muscle testing, MG-ADL). Items are related to ptosis, double vision, eye closure, talking, chewing, swallowing, breathing, neck flexion, shoulder abduction, and hip flexion. Each item is scored on an ordinal scale with four possible categories and weighted. The total score ranges from 0 to 50, with higher scores indicating more severe impairments.

L199: ## **Myasthenia Gravis Symptoms PRO (MGSPRO)**
L200: The MG Symptoms PRO (MGSPRO; Table 11) is a 42-item PRO instrument measuring five symptom concepts of MG including ocular muscle weakness (5 items), bulbar muscle weakness (10 items), respiratory muscle weakness (3 items), physical fatigue (15 items), and muscle weakness fatigability (9 items). The MGSPRO instrument was developed based on interviews with 96 subjects with MG and quantitative psychometric analysis of data from a total of 273 subjects with MG (30 from the qualitative research sample, 43 from the phase 2 study, MG0002, and 200 from MG0003). The Applicant has included change from baseline to day 43 in three MGSPRO domains in the Study MG0003: muscle weakness fatigability, physical fatigue, and bulbar muscle weakness as secondary efficacy endpoints.
L201: The MGSPRO was administered weekly with a recall period of 7 days for each item during the double-blind treatment period. The MG0003 study endpoints were defined using the MG Symptoms PRO scale scores which are simple linear transformation of the raw score on a 0 to 100 range. The item-level raw scores were summed first and the raw total score subsequently transformed to 0 to 100. A scale score was calculated if more than 70% of items were responded to minimize missing items data. In Study MG0003, 1307 out of 1329 assessments (98.3%) were considered completed, nine (0.7%) included one missing item, whereas 7 (0.5%) had full questionnaire missing. The missing data were observed across a variety of items of the MGSPRO instrument.
L202: The Applicant provided anchor based empirical cumulative distribution function and probability distribution function for a patient global impression of severity scale (PGI-S) and global impression of change scale, as per the Division's recommendations in the Type B end-of-phase 2 meeting on May 22, 2019.
L203: 3 Page(s) have been Withheld in Full as b4 (CCI/TS) immediately following this page
L204: **Reviewer's Comments:** Previously, the Division of Neurology 1 (DN1) and the Clinical Outcome Assessment review team provided recommendations regarding MGSPRO in the pre-IND meeting minutes dated December 9, 2016, Type B end-of-phase 2 meeting minutes dated June 14, 2019, and Type C written responses on November 3, 2020 (IND132407). Specifically, DN1 expressed concerns regarding the 7-day recall period, the length of the instrument and concept redundancy which increases burden to subjects and missing data. DN1 recommended that the Applicant conduct exit interviews which may provide an opportunity to obtain a subject's input on what they consider to be a meaningful change. However, the recall period, the instrument redundancy, and the instrument items length were unchanged, and no exit interviews were conducted for the study.
L205: ## 6.2.2.2. Eligibility Criteria, Study MG0003
L206: **Reviewer's Comments:** The criteria listed below from the submitted protocol appear adequate to enroll subjects with gMG representative of the U.S. population.
L207: ## Inclusion Criteria
L208: To participate in this study, subjects were required to meet the following eligibility criteria:
L209: - Male or female subjects aged \ge 18 years
- Diagnosis of gMG based on history and previous evaluations
- Positive record of anti-AChR or anti-MuSK autoantibodies at screening (visit 1). The presence of autoantibodies may be confirmed with repeat testing at visit 1.
- Myasthenia Gravis Foundation of America (MGFA) Class II to Iva at visit 1. The MGFA classification is shown in Figure 2.
- A MG-ADL score of at least 3 (with \ge 3 points from nonocular symptom) AND a QMG score of at least 11 at visit 1 and at baseline (visit 2).
- Being considered for additional treatment such as IVIg or PEX by the investigator.
- Bodyweight \ge 35 kg at visit 1.
- Highly effective contraception
- Able to give informed consent
L210: ## **Figure 2. Myasthenia Gravis Foundation of America (MGFA) Classification**
L211: ## **MGFA Clinical Classification**
L212: **Class I:** Any ocular muscle weakness; may have weakness of eye closure. All other muscle strength is normal.
L213: **Class II:** Mild weakness affecting muscles other than ocular muscles; may also have ocular muscle weakness of any severity.
L214: A. IIa. Predominantly affecting limb, axial muscles, or both. May also have lesser involvement of oropharyngeal muscles.
B. IIb. Predominantly affecting oropharyngeal, respiratory muscles, or both. May also have lesser or equal involvement of limb, axial muscles, or both.
L215: **Class III:** Moderate weakness affecting muscles other than ocular muscles; may also have ocular muscle weakness of any severity.
L216: A. IIIa. Predominantly affecting limb, axial muscles, or both. May also have lesser involvement of oropharyngeal muscles.
B. IIIb. Predominantly affecting oropharyngeal, respiratory muscles, or both. May also have lesser or equal involvement of limb, axial muscles, or both.
L217: **Class IV:** Severe weakness affecting muscles other than ocular muscles; may also have ocular muscle weakness of any severity.
L218: A. IVa. Predominantly affecting limb, axial muscles, or both. May also have lesser involvement of oropharyngeal muscles.
B. IVb. Predominantly affecting oropharyngeal, respiratory muscles, or both. May also have lesser or equal involvement of limb, axial muscles, or both.
L219: **Class V:** Defined as intubation, with or without mechanical ventilation, except when employed during routine postoperative management. The use of a feeding tube without intubation places the patient in class IVb.
L220: Source: Myasthenia Gravis Foundation of America Task Force
Abbreviations: MGFA, Myasthenia Gravis Foundation of America
L221: ## **Key Exclusion Criteria**
L222: Subjects meeting any of the following criteria were not eligible for the study:
L223: - Has a severe (defined as grade 3 on the MG-ADL scale) weakness affecting oropharyngeal or respiratory muscles, or who has myasthenic crisis or impending crisis at visit 1 or visit 2.
- Has the following laboratory abnormalities:Serum total IgG level \le 5.5 g/L.
- Absolute neutrophil count &lt;1500 cells/mm^3.
- Significant electrocardiogram findings as assessed by the investigator.
- Fridericia corrected QT interval &gt;450 msec (for male subjects) or corrected QT interval &gt;470 msec (for female subjects) or corrected QT interval &gt;480 msec in subjects with bundle branch block.
- Glomerular filtration rate (GFR) less than 45 mL/min/1.73m^2 at visit 1.
L224: - Alanine transaminase (ALT), aspartate aminotransferase (AST), or alkaline phosphatase (ALP) are &gt;3\times upper limit of normal (ULN), or bilirubin &gt;1.5\times ULN (isolated bilirubin &gt;1.5\times ULN is acceptable if bilirubin is fractionated and direct bilirubin &lt;35\%).
- Presence of hepatitis B surface antigen at visit 1.
- Positive hepatitis C antibody or ribonucleic acid test result at visit 1 or within 3 months prior to starting study treatment.
- Positive for human immunodeficiency virus.
- Current unstable liver or biliary disease per the investigator assessment defined by the presence of ascites, encephalopathy, coagulopathy, hypoalbuminemia, esophageal or gastric varices, persistent jaundice, or cirrhosis. Note: with exception of stable hepatobiliary conditions (including Gilbert's syndrome, asymptomatic gallstones).
- Has a current or medical history of primary immunodeficiency.
- Has active neoplastic disease or history of neoplastic disease within 5 years of study entry prior to visit 1 (except for basal or squamous cell carcinoma of the skin or carcinoma in situ of the uterine cervix that have been definitively treated with standard of care approaches).
- Any medical or psychiatric condition that in the opinion of investigator may jeopardize or would compromise the subject's ability to participate in the study.
- A history of alcohol or substance use disorder within 12 months before visit 1.
- A clinically relevant active infection (e.g., sepsis, pneumonia, or abscess) in the opinion of the investigator, or had a serious infection (resulting in hospitalization or requiring parenteral antibiotic treatment) within 6 weeks prior to the first dose of investigational medicinal product.
- A known tuberculosis (TB) infection, at high risk of acquiring TB infection, or latent tuberculosis infection, or current/history of nontuberculous mycobacterial infection.
- Has been treated with prohibited immunosuppressants, biologics, or other therapies (Table 12). Permitted concomitant MG medications are listed in Table 13.
- Have received a live vaccination within 8 weeks prior to visit 2; or intends to have a live vaccination during the course of the study or within 8 weeks following the final dose of investigational medicinal product.
- Has previously received rozanolixizumab drug product.
- Has prior treatment with rituximab in the 6 months prior to visit 2 or study subject has prior treatment with rituximab in the 12 months prior to visit 2 and B cells monitoring have shown they did not return to normal range.
- Had a thymectomy in the past 6 months or a thymoma at any time that required chemotherapy and/or radiotherapy prior to visit 1.
- Has any of the following active GI disorders: inflammatory bowel disease, or GI ulceration or diverticulitis.
L225: - Has experienced hypersensitivity reaction after exposure to other anti-FcRn drugs.
- Has a history of a solid organ transplant or hematopoietic stem cell/marrow transplant

L236: ## **Compliance With Good Clinical Practice, Study MG0003**
L237: Study execution, protocol adherence, and data integrity appear adequate. The data generated are interpretable. Union Chimique Belge (UCB), Inc. attested to good clinical practices such as human subject protection, institutional review board compliance, and clinical investigator obligations including financial disclosures as defined in International Council on Harmonisation-Good Clinical Practice (ICH-GCP), and in accordance with the ethical principles that have their origin in the Declaration of Helsinki.
L238: ## **6.2.2.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Study MG0003**
L239: The primary efficacy analysis compared rozanolixizumab of ≈7 mg/kg dose and rozanolixizumab of ≈10 mg/kg dose to placebo following a 2-stage group sequential design using a combination test. The final version of the protocol of Study MG0003 was dated
L240: February 23, 2021. The final version of statistical analysis plan (SAP) of Study MG0003 was dated November 10, 2021.
L241: The analysis was conducted on the randomized set, consisting of all study subjects who were randomized. The subjects were analyzed using the treatment assigned instead of the actual treatment received. The familywise type I error rate was controlled at a 2-sided alpha level of 0.05 by using a parallel gatekeeping testing procedure with a truncated Hochberg test.
L242: An IDMC monitored the ongoing safety and efficacy of the study to make recommendations on futility, selection of the “best dose” of rozanolixizumab, and sample size re-estimation. The interim analysis was performed on 92 enrolled study subjects with a data cutoff date of April 13, 2021. The IDMC recommended that the study remained unchanged, with both the \approx 7 mg/kg and \approx 10 mg/kg doses carried forward. The recommended sample size for the second stage of the study was 90 subjects (i.e., 30 subjects per treatment group). The primary efficacy analysis of the primary endpoint was conducted with a stagewise mixed model repeated measures (MMRM) using an inverse-normal method of combining independent stagewise p-values. The model included treatment group, baseline MG-ADL score, region, stratification factors including AChR(+/−) and MuSK(+/−), and treatment group by day (interaction term) as fixed factors and study subject as a random effect. The MMRM included days 8, 15, 22, 29, 36 and 43. An unstructured covariance matrix was used for the repeated measures. Missing data were handled under a missing at random assumption using a hypothetical and treatment policy strategy to handle intercurrent events (ICEs). The combined p-value (2-sided) for the two stages was based on the stagewise inverse-normal combination of independent stagewise p-values using the Lehmaner and Wassmer method. The multiplicity adjusted 95% CIs were provided for the differences in least squares (LS) mean change from baseline in MG-ADL score to day 43 (visit 10) between rozanolixizumab groups and placebo. The lower bound of the 2-sided 95% repeated confidence interval for a given treatment group was found as the smallest value for which the multiplicity-adjusted combination test yields nonrejection at a two-sided alpha =5%. The upper bound of the confidence interval was found analogously.
L243: The key secondary endpoints of change from baseline to day 43 in MG-C score, change from baseline to day 43 in QMG score, and changes from baseline to day 43 in MGSPRO domains muscle weakness fatigability score, physical fatigue score and bulbar symptoms score were analyzed using the same approach as that of the primary analysis of the primary endpoint.
L244: ## 6.2.2.4. Results of Analyses, Study MG0003
L245: This section summarizes the subject disposition, baseline demographics, clinical characteristics, and primary and key secondary efficacy results to support the efficacy of rozanolixizumab.
L246: ## 6.2.2.4.1. Disposition, Baseline Demographics and Baseline Clinical Characteristics
L247: ## Disposition
L248: Subject disposition information for the randomized set is summarized in Table 14. The percentage of the total 200 study subjects who completed the study was 64.0% (n=128). Seventy-
L249: two subjects (36.0%) discontinued the study permanently; all discontinuations occurred during the coronavirus disease of 2019 (COVID-19) pandemic. The discontinuation percentages were similar across the 3 treatment groups, 37.3% in placebo group, 34.8% in rozanolixizumab of \approx7 mg/kg dose group and 35.8% in rozanolixizumab of \approx10 mg/kg dose group. The most common reasons for discontinuation were mandatory withdrawal and roll over to MG0004 and MG0007.

L254: ## Baseline Demographics and Clinical Characteristics
L255: Demographics for the Study MG0003 are described in the following tables, based on the submitted data (Table 15, Table 16, and Table 17). Study MG0003 enrolled 200 subjects, almost all during the COVID-19 pandemic (183 [91.5%]; and the rest (17 [8.5%]) during the pre-COVID-19 pandemic period. One hundred twenty (60.5%) were female. This sex imbalance is consistent with the natural history of the disease, as described in Section 2.1. There were 66 subjects randomized to the rozanolixizumab \approx7 mg/kg group, and 67 subjects each to the rozanolixizumab \approx10 mg/kg and placebo groups. There was a higher proportion of female study subjects in the placebo group (70.2%) compared with the rozanolixizumab groups (\approx7 mg/kg [59.1%] and \approx10 mg/kg [52.2%]). There were few subjects in the &lt;50 kg body weight category at baseline (6%) and fewer in the \approx10 mg/kg dose group compared to the \approx7 mg/kg and placebo groups (1.5% versus 10.6% and 6%, respectively). At baseline, age and race were adequately balanced between the placebo and rozanolixizumab groups. Subjects 65 years and older comprised 24.5% of total and were adequately balanced between the treatment arms. The majority (89.5%) of subjects were anti-AChR antibody positive and the remaining 10.5% of subjects were anti-MuSK autoantibody positive. At baseline, the disease severity and the localization of symptoms, as indicated by the Myasthenia Gravis Foundation of America (MGFA) classification, were similar between the subjects randomized to rozanolixizumab or placebo. The subjects had moderate to severe gMG with a larger proportion of the randomized subjects in the MGFA class III and IV (61%), and the rest in the MGFA class II (39%). The MG-
L256: ADL and QMG scores, and disease duration at baseline were balanced between the placebo and rozanolixizumab groups. The mean MG-ADL score was 8.3, the mean QMG score was 15.6, the mean disease duration was 8.6 years, and 29.5% of the randomized subjects had a history of MG crisis in the past. The treatment groups were balanced with regard to standard of care including the use of steroids, nonsteroidal immunosuppressant drugs, and acetylcholine esterase inhibitor drugs. Relatively fewer subjects in the rozanolixizumab \approx10 mg/kg group had undergone thymectomy compared with the \approx7 mg/kg and placebo groups (29.9% versus 48.5% and 46.3%, respectively). The presence of medical conditions other than gMG was similar between the rozanolixizumab and placebo groups. The most frequent medical comorbidity was systemic hypertension (31.5%). Headaches were reported in only 10% of total subjects with a half of them reporting headaches as migraines at baseline (Table 17)
L257: **Reviewer's Comments:** Overall, there is an acceptable balance of demographic characteristics between the placebo and rozanolixizumab treatment groups that adequately represents the demographics of the intended subject population with moderate to severe gMG who are seropositive for anti-AChR or anti-MuSK antibodies. The proportion of female subjects and subjects with anti-MuSK antibodies is consistent with the disease prevalence in the intended patient population (Gilhus et al. 2019). Note that anti-MuSK antibody seropositive subjects were not included in the previous clinical studies that led to approval of eculizumab and ravulizumab in gMG. For efgartigimod, six subjects in the anti-AChR antibody negative group had anti-MuSK antibodies in the study (three in active arm, three in placebo arm); however, efficacy in the anti-MuSK subgroup was not established in that study. A majority (95.5%) of the subjects in Study MG003 were on concomitant medications including, acetylcholine esterase inhibitor drugs, steroids, and nonsteroidal immunosuppressant drugs with balanced distribution across treatment groups.
L258: ## **Protocol Deviations, Study MG0003**
L259: No subjects were excluded from any analysis due to protocol deviation. A total of 22 subjects (11%; 21 during the COVID-19 pandemic [10.5%]) had an important protocol deviation including, procedural noncompliance (n=8 [4%]), inclusion criteria deviation and incorrect treatment dose (n=4 [2%]) each), withdrawal criteria deviation and prohibited concomitant medication use (n=3 [1.5%] each), and exclusion criteria deviation (n=2 [1%]).
L260: **Reviewer's Comments:** The protocol deviations affected 11% of the randomized subjects and none were excluded from any analysis. They were unlikely to have had an impact on the efficacy and safety analyses.
L261: ## **Impact of the COVID-19 Pandemic on Study MG0003**
L262: A majority of the subjects enrolled (91.5%) were randomized during the COVID-19 pandemic (the first laboratory-confirmed case of COVID-19 in the United States was January 20, 2020; the WHO declared a pandemic on March 11, 2020). The recruitment was paused due to the pandemic from March 20, 2020, to June 3, 2020, but the randomized subjects continued the study. The pandemic impacted 13 subjects (6.5%) and a total of 33 visits including, 12 visits due to confirmed COVID-19 infection (n=6 visits not done, n=4 visits noted as temporary discontinuation of study drug, and n=2 visits performed by telephone).
L263: **Reviewer's Comments:** The impact of the COVID-19 pandemic was minor and affected a relatively small number of the subjects. There were no protocol deviations related to COVID-19. The first subject was randomized [REDACTED](b) (6), and the last study drug infusion was [REDACTED](b) (6), with the final study visit [REDACTED](b) (6). The interim analysis was April 13, 2021, of 92 subjects who had completed the 6-week treatment period.

L280: ## 6.2.2.4.2. Primary and Key Secondary Efficacy Results
L281: ## Primary Efficacy Endpoint
L282: The Applicant's primary efficacy results were confirmed by the statistical reviewer. The results demonstrated superiority of rozanolixizumab of ≈7 mg/kg dose level and rozanolixizumab of ≈10 mg/kg dose level compared to placebo in improvement from baseline in MG-ADL score to day 43 in study subjects with gMG.
L283: The difference in least squares (LS) mean change from baseline in MG-ADL score between rozanolixizumab of ≈7 mg/kg dose level group and placebo group was -2.59 (95% CI: -4.09, -1.25; p&lt;0.001) in favor of rozanolixizumab.
L284: The difference in least squares (LS) mean change from baseline in MG-ADL score between rozanolixizumab of ≈10 mg/kg dose level group and placebo group was -2.62 (95% CI: -3.99, -1.16; p&lt;0.001) in favor of rozanolixizumab.
L285: All results in **Table 18** below were from the combined MMRM, except Cis and p-values, which were based on the stagewise inverse-normal combination using the Lehmacer and Wassmer method.

L291: The following sensitivity analyses were repeated for the primary endpoint:
L292: - Sensitivity Analysis #1: the full analysis set (FAS) using the hypothetical and treatment policy strategy to handle intercurrent events (ICEs)
- Sensitivity Analysis #2: the randomized set using a jump-to-reference approach for imputation of missing data and using the hypothetical and treatment policy strategy to handle ICEs
- Sensitivity Analysis #3: the subgroup of the randomized set who received all 6 subcutaneous doses using hypothetical and treatment policy strategy to handle ICEs
- Sensitivity Analysis #4: the subgroup of the FAS excluding confirmed COVID-19 cases, using treatment policy strategy to handle ICEs.
L293: The following supplementary analyses were repeated for the primary endpoint:
L294: - Supplementary Analysis #1: the randomized set using the composite strategy to handle the occurrence of an intercurrent event as treatment failures and using a trimmed mean approach to impute missing data
- Supplementary Analysis #2: the randomized set using the treatment policy strategy to handle ICEs.
L295: Results of sensitivity analyses and supplementary analyses are in **Table 19**. The results of the prespecified sensitivity analyses and supplementary analyses were consistent with the primary analysis results.
